(C.32)

Finding the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the transfer matrix, it can be written as

\begin{pmatrix}\omega&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\lambda_{2}\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\lambda_{2}\\
-1&\lambda_{1}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(C.33)

where
\lambda_{1,2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\omega\pm\sqrt{\omega^{2}-4}\right)
. This allows us to write the solution for any
n
in terms of
\psi_{0}
and
\lambda_{1,2}
, as follows:

\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{n}\\
\psi_{n-1}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\lambda_{2}\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}^{n}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\lambda_{2}\\
-1&\lambda_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{0}\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{\psi_{0}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}^{n+1}-\lambda_{2}^{n+1}\\
\lambda_{1}^{n}-\lambda_{2}^{n}\end{pmatrix}~.

(C.34)

Now we can use the other boundary condition, namely
\psi_{K}=0
:

\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\psi_{K-1}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{\psi_{0}}{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}^{K+1}-\lambda_{2}^{K+1}\\
\lambda_{1}^{K}-\lambda_{2}^{K}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(C.35)

from which we extract the condition
\lambda_{1}^{K+1}-\lambda_{2}^{K+1}=0
, which in terms of
\omega
reads

\left(\omega+\sqrt{\omega^{2}-4}\right)^{K+1}-\left(\omega-\sqrt{\omega^{2}-4}\right)^{K+1}=0~.

(C.36)

A family of solutions is

\omega_{i}=2\cos\left(\frac{\pi(i+1)}{K+1}\right),\hskip 9.24994pti=0,1,2,\dots,K-1~.

(C.37)

It is also clear now from (
C.34
), that the eigenvector element
\psi_{ni}
for eigenvalue
\omega_{i}
is:

\centering\psi_{ni}=\psi_{0i}\frac{\sin\left(\frac{(n+1)(i+1)}{K+1}\pi\right)}{\sin\left(\frac{i+1}{K+1}\pi\right)}\equiv C_{i}\sin\left(\frac{(n+1)(i+1)}{K+1}\pi\right),\;\;n,i=0,1,2,...,K-1\,,\@add@centering

(C.38)

where we have absorbed the denominator in the normalization constant because it does not depend on
n
(recall that the label
i
designates the eigenstate).
C_{i}
can now be fixed from normalization, and the normalized eigenstates are

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{0i}\\
\psi_{1i}\\
\vdots\\
\psi_{ni}\\
\vdots\\
\psi_{K-1,i}\end{pmatrix}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{K+1}}\begin{pmatrix}\sin\frac{(i+1)\pi}{K+1}\\
\sin\frac{2(i+1)\pi}{K+1}\\
\vdots\\
\sin\frac{(n+1)(i+1)\pi}{K+1}\\
\vdots\\
\sin\frac{K(i+1)\pi}{K+1}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 9.24994pti=0,1,2,\dots,K-1~.

(C.39)

In terms of Krylov elements, we found that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{O}_{n}|\omega_{i})=\psi_{ni}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{K+1}}\sin\frac{(n+1)(i+1)\pi}{K+1}\hskip 9.24994pti,n=0,1,\dots,K-1\,,

(C.40)

from which we can compute K-complexity for the pure no-disorder system:

\displaystyle\overline{C_{K}}=\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}|(\mathcal{O}_{0}|\omega_{i})|^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{K-1}n\,|(\mathcal{O}_{n}|\omega_{i})|^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{K-1}n\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\psi_{0i}^{2}\psi_{ni}^{2}~.

(C.41)

The transition probability (
4.19
) is
1
1

1

To arrive at this result we used
\sum_{n=0}^{N}e^{\frac{2i\pi mn}{N+1}}=\frac{1-e^{2i\pi m}}{1-e^{\frac{2i\pi m}{N+1}}}=\begin{cases}0,&(N+1)\text{ not }|m\\
N+1,&(N+1)|m\end{cases}
. See
[
499
]
.

\displaystyle Q_{0n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2}{K+1}\right)^{2}\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\sin^{2}\frac{(i+1)\pi}{K+1}\sin^{2}\frac{(n+1)(i+1)\pi}{K+1}

(C.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K+1}\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{n0}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{n,K-1}\right)

and the time-averaged K-complexity is given by,

\displaystyle\overline{C_{K}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K+1}\sum_{n=0}^{K-1}n\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{n0}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{n,K-1}\right)

(C.43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{K(K-1)}{2(K+1)}+0+\frac{K-1}{2(K+1)}=\frac{K}{2}\times O(1)+O(1)\sim\frac{K}{2}~.

This analytical result shows that for a flat and smooth Lanczos-sequence K-complexity will saturate at
K/2
.

C.4
Connected part of autocorrelation function and saturation value of K-complexity

This Appendix analyses the impact of the operator one-point function on the saturation value of K-complexity at late times.
Let us first remind how the operator one-point function dominates the two-point function plateau. We shall do so by assuming that the operator satisfies the Eigenstate Thermalization Hypothesis (ETH).
Consider a hermitian normalized operator whose elements in the energy basis are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}O_{ab}|E_{a}\rangle\langle E_{b}|

(C.44)

where
D
is the Hilbert space dimension. Note that in (
C.44
) the operator matrix elements in the energy basis are defined such that
O_{ab}=\langle E_{a}|\mathcal{O}|E_{b}\rangle
.
With this convention, the ETH Anstatz takes the usual form; suppressing energy dependence in the matrix elements of the Ansatz (since we are interested in order-of-magnitude estimates), it boils down to the RMT operator Ansatz:

\centering O_{ab}=O\delta_{ab}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}r_{ab},\@add@centering

(C.45)

where
O
gives (up to non-perturbative corrections) the one-point function of the operator and is taken not to scale with
D
, and the matrix
(r_{ab})
is drawn from a Gaussian ensemble with unit variance (and hence the elements
r_{ab}
are also of order
D^{0}
). Note that the operator (
C.44
) with the matrix elements given by (
C.45
) is normalized
2
2

2

By this, we mean that the norm of the operator whose matrix elements satisfy (
C.45
) does not scale with
D
.
according to the operator inner product

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{O}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{D}\mathrm{Tr}\Big[\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}\Big]=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|O_{ab}|^{2}=1.

(C.46)

The autocorrelation function is given by

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}(t)\big\rangle=\frac{1}{D}\mathrm{Tr}\Big[\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}(t)\Big]=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|O_{ab}|^{2}e^{i(E_{a}-E_{b})t}~.

(C.47)

Due to normalization of the operator (
C.46
), the two-point function (
C.47
) starts at
1
, i.e.
\phi_{0}(0)=1
. The Ansatz (
C.45
) has some implications on the late-time behavior of
\phi_{0}(t)
, which we can study by performing a long-time average:

\centering\overline{\phi_{0}}:=\lim_{T\to+\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}dt\,\phi_{0}(t).\@add@centering

(C.48)

We can now use that, for
\omega\neq 0
:

\centering\lim_{T\to+\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}dt\,e^{i\omega t}=\lim_{T\to+\infty}\frac{1}{T}\left.\left[\frac{e^{i\omega t}}{i\omega}\right]\right|_{t=0}^{T}=\frac{1}{i\omega}\lim_{T\to+\infty}\frac{e^{i\omega T}-1}{T}=0.\@add@centering

(C.49)

With this, assuming no exact degeneracies in the energy spectrum, the long-time average of (
C.47
) eliminates the contribution of the off-diagonal matrix elements and yields:

\centering\overline{\phi_{0}}=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a=1}^{D}|O_{aa}|^{2}=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a=1}^{D}\left(O+\frac{r_{aa}}{\sqrt{D}}\right)^{2}=O^{2}+\frac{2O}{D^{3/2}}\sum_{a=1}^{D}r_{aa}+\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a=1}^{D}r_{aa}^{2},\@add@centering

(C.50)

where in the second equality we have used the Ansatz (
C.45
). We thus conclude that the long-time average of the two-point function is dominated by the square of the one-point function. This fact is also in qualitative agreement with large-N factorization, i.e. in the thermodynamic limit the two-point function becomes disconnected at late times.

In order to probe spectral correlations we can choose to subtract explicitly the one-point function squared from the auto-correlation function (
C.47
), which defines the so-called connected two-point function:

\centering\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t):=\Bigg\langle\bigg(\mathcal{O}-\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\bigg)\bigg(\mathcal{O}(t)-\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle\bigg)\Bigg\rangle=\big\langle\mathcal{O}\mathcal{O}(t)\big\rangle-\big\langle\mathcal{O}\big\rangle^{2},\@add@centering

(C.51)

where, to alleviate notational crowding, we have implicitly assumed that
\mathcal{O}
is hermitian, and we have made use of the fact that the one-point function is time-independent,
\big\langle\mathcal{O}(t)\big\rangle=\big\langle\mathcal{O}\big\rangle
. Again, making use of the expression of the operator
\mathcal{O}
in the energy basis, we can write (
C.51
) as:

\centering\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|O_{ab}|^{2}e^{it(E_{a}-E_{b})}\,-\,\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}O_{aa}O_{bb},\@add@centering

(C.52)

where we have used that
\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle=\frac{1}{D}\text{Tr}[\mathcal{O}]
is the infinite-temperature one-point function of the operator
\mathcal{O}
. As defined in (
C.51
), the connected two-point function is not normalized so that its value at
t=0
is exactly one, but this is not important because we can still prove that
\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t=0)
is of order one, i.e. its value does not scale with
D
:

\centering\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t=0)=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|O_{ab}|^{2}-\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=2}^{D}O_{aa}O_{bb}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|r_{ab}|^{2}-\frac{1}{D}\left\{\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}r_{aa}r_{bb}\right\},\@add@centering

(C.53)

as can be seen plugging in the Ansatz (
C.45
). We note that the leading term in (
C.53
) is the first term of the last expression, consisting of a sum of
D^{2}
numbers of order one, divided by a
D^{2}
factor, and hence
\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t=0)
is a number of order
D^{0}
.

Now, we can estimate the height of the late-time plateau by computing the long-time average of the connected two-point function:

\centering\overline{\phi_{0}^{(c)}}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}dt\,\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a=1}^{D}O_{aa}^{2}-\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}O_{aa}O_{bb}.\@add@centering

(C.54)

Plugging the Ansatz (
C.45
) in (
C.54
) we again find that the terms involving the order-one quantity
O
cancel out, yielding:

\centering\overline{\phi_{0}^{(c)}}=\frac{1}{D}\left\{\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a=1}^{D}r_{aa}^{2}+\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}r_{aa}r_{bb}\right\}.\@add@centering

(C.55)

The quantity inside the braces is of order
D^{0}
. We thus conclude that the connected two-point function has a long-time average of order
\frac{1}{D}
, and that this is deduced from the ETH-like Ansatz (
C.45
). To argue that
\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)
actually plateaus at
\frac{1}{D}
, one should prove that its long time variance is (exponentially) suppressed
3
3

3

We shall refer to quantities of order
\frac{1}{D}
or smaller as
exponentially suppressed
because the Hilbert space dimension is typically exponential in the number of degrees of freedom
S
of the system, i.e.
D\sim e^{S}
.
, so that the function remains close to its long-time average at late times. We shall do that later, when studying the long time average of the square of the two-point function. But before that, we can note that there was a simpler way to derive the previous results, by defining a new operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
obtained by subtracting the one-point function from the initial operator
\mathcal{O}
:

\centering\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{O}-\mathds{1}\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle=\mathcal{O}-\frac{1}{D}\text{Tr}[\mathcal{O}]\mathds{1}.\@add@centering

(C.56)

Using the operator Ansatz for
\mathcal{O}
given in (
C.45
), we note that the matrix elements of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
in the energy basis are given by:

\centering\widetilde{O}_{ab}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}\widetilde{r}_{ab},\@add@centering

(C.57)

where all
\widetilde{r}_{ab}
are of order one and the matrix
\widetilde{R}\equiv\left(\widetilde{r}_{ab}\right)
is related to the matrix
R\equiv\left(r_{ab}\right)
through:

\centering\widetilde{R}=R-\mathds{1}\langle R\rangle.\@add@centering

(C.58)

In particular:

\centering\widetilde{r}_{ab}=r_{ab}-\frac{\delta_{ab}}{D}\sum_{c=1}^{D}r_{cc},\@add@centering

(C.59)

from where it is apparent that all
\widetilde{r}_{ab}
are of order one and that they follow the exact constraint
\text{Tr}[\widetilde{R}]=\sum_{a=1}^{D}\widetilde{r}_{aa}=0
.

This operator redefinition is useful because we immediately note that the connected two-point function of
\mathcal{O}
is identically equal to the full two-point function of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
:

\centering\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)=\big\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(t)\big\rangle.\@add@centering

(C.60)

And thus, using the expression of
\widetilde{O}
in the energy basis (
C.57
) it is straightforward to see that:

\centering\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|\widetilde{O}_{ab}|^{2}e^{it(E_{a}-E_{b})}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|\widetilde{r}_{ab}|^{2}e^{it(E_{a}-E_{b})},\@add@centering

(C.61)

from where it is immediate that
\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t=0)\sim D^{0}
and that
\overline{\phi_{0}^{(c)}}\sim\frac{1}{D}
.

A similar argument can now be applied to transition probabilities on the Krylov chain.
The long-time average of the transition probability is given by (
4.19
)

\displaystyle Q_{0n}:=\overline{|\phi_{n}|^{2}}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|\phi_{n}(t)|^{2}dt~.

(C.62)

which for
n=0
takes the form

\displaystyle Q_{00}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\Big[\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|O_{aa}|^{2}|O_{bb}|^{2}+\sum_{a\neq b=1}^{D}|O_{ab}|^{4}\Big]~.

(C.63)

From the Ansatz (
C.45
) we find that:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}|O_{aa}|^{2}|O_{bb}|^{2}\sim O^{4}+\mathit{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right).

(C.64)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a\neq b=1}^{D}|O_{ab}|^{4}\sim\mathit{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right).

(C.65)

We thus find that the long-time-average of the square of the autocorrelation function behaves like

\displaystyle\overline{|\phi_{0}|^{2}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle O(1)+O\left(\frac{1}{D}\right).

(C.66)

The time averaged transition probability
Q_{00}
takes an order-one value controlled by the one-point function. In order to see an exponentially suppressed plateau, we again need to work with the connected two-point function
\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)
, and the associated probability
P_{00}^{(c)}(t):=\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)^{2}
(note that the two-point function is always real provided that the operator is hermitian, which we assume), whose long-time average we shall denote
Q_{00}^{(c)}
. As we showed in (
C.53
),
\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t=0)\sim 1
, and therefore
P_{00}^{(c)}(t=0)\sim 1
. Likewise,
Q_{00}^{(c)}
can be expressed in terms of the matrix elements of the traceless operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
:

\centering Q_{00}^{(c)}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}\left[\widetilde{O}_{aa}^{2}\widetilde{O}_{bb}^{2}+|\widetilde{O}_{ab}|^{4}\right]=\frac{1}{D^{4}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{D}\left[\widetilde{r}_{aa}^{2}\widetilde{r}_{bb}^{2}+|\widetilde{r}_{ab}|^{4}\right]\sim\frac{D^{2}}{D^{4}}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}.\@add@centering

(C.67)

And hence
P_{00}^{(c)}(t)
plateaus
4
4

4

Actually, in order to show that
P_{00}^{(c)}(t)
plateaus at the long-time average
Q_{00}^{(c)}
, we should also show that the long-time variance of
P_{00}^{(c)}(t)
is suppressed, otherwise a strongly oscillating function could still be compatible with the long-time average prediction. This proof is doable (even though cumbersome), but
P_{00}^{(c)}(t)\sim e^{-2S}
at late times seems to hold for ETH operators in chaotic systems like cSYK
4
according to numerical checks.
at late times at
\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sim e^{-2S}
. Incidentally, note that
Q_{00}^{(c)}
gives the long-time variance of
\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)
, and hence showing that (
C.67
) is suppressed concludes the proof that the connected two-point function is close to the plateau value at late times, as anticipated above.

C.4.1
Example: Hopping vs number operator in cSYK
4

The work on cSYK
4

[
271
]
, studied the K-complexity of hopping operators,
h_{ij}=c_{i}^{\dagger}c_{j}+h.c
. These operators have a zero one-point function, and hence their two-point function is connected. However, non-universal effects due to a non-zero one-point function can be probed if we consider, for example, an on-site number operator
n_{i}=c_{i}^{\dagger}c_{i}
. Indeed, since in cSYK
4
we work in fixed occupation sectors, the one-point functions of the on-site number operators are constrained by the relation:

\centering N=\sum_{i=1}^{L}n_{i}\;\Longrightarrow\;\langle N\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{L}\langle n_{i}\rangle,\@add@centering

(C.68)

where
N
is the total number operator and
L
is the number of sites (or rather, the number of complex fermions). Hence, (
C.68
) together with the fact that
\langle n_{i}\rangle\geq 0
implies that at least one on-site number operator needs to have a non-zero expectation value whenever
\langle N\rangle>0
. In fact, the chaotic character of cSYK
4
seems to distribute equally the expectation value accross all the
n_{i}
, and given a fixed occupation sector we can thus estimate
\langle n_{i}\rangle\sim\frac{\langle N\rangle}{L}
for all
i=1,...,L
, i.e. the one-point function equals the filling ratio.

This non-zero one-point function controls the averaged transition probability
Q_{00}
, which becomes of order one in system size and has the effect of lowering the K-complexity saturation value in (
4.20
). Figure
C.2
illustrates this claim.

Figure C.2:
Averaged transition probabilities
Q_{0n}
in cSYK
4
for the hopping operator and both the full and the subtracted version of the number operator. Vertical lines mark the estimated K-complexity saturation value. For this system size,
K=15751\sim 10^{4}
, and we observe that
Q_{00}\sim 1
for the full number operator, signaling that indeed the one-point function dominates the late-time regime of the transition probability. The rest of the
Q_{0n}
with
n>1
for the full number operator seem to be rather uniformly distributed, but with a lower value due to the constraint
\sum_{n=0}^{K-1}Q_{0n}=1
. This eventually enforces a complexity saturation value of
\sim 0.2K
, much below the naively expected
\sim\frac{K}{2}
, which the hopping operator does display. Conversely, both for the hopping operator and for the subtracted number operator, which have a zero one-point function by construction, all the transition probabilities are rather uniformly distributed around
\frac{1}{K}
, yielding a K-complexity saturation value much closer to
\frac{K}{2}
.

In order to avoid this, we can seed the Lanczos algorithm with the subtracted version of the operator,
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
, which by construction has a zero one-point function. Following the usual arguments
[
290
,
348
]
, we find that the Lanczos coefficients
\widetilde{b}_{n}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the moments
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}
of the connected two-point function of
\mathcal{O}
,
\phi_{0}^{(c)}(t)
, and that the K-complexity long-time average is given by:

\centering\overline{C_{K}}=\sum_{n=0}^{K-1}n\widetilde{Q_{0n}},\@add@centering

(C.69)

where
\widetilde{Q_{00}}=Q_{00}^{(c)}
. Therefore, for an ETH operator this last quantity will be exponentially suppressed, hence not competing with the other uniformly distributed
\widetilde{Q_{0n}}
with
n>0
and allowing for a K-complexity saturation value closer to
K/2
. This is illustrated in Figure
C.2
.

C.4.2
Role of the one-point function in XXZ

This may raise some concern regarding previous work in XXZ
[
272
]
, as the operators used in that case were on-site Pauli sigma matrices, whose one-point function in fixed-magnetization Hilbert space sectors are also constrained by the value of the total magnetization in the given sector, through:

\centering S^{z}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sigma_{n}^{z}\;\longrightarrow\;\langle S^{z}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle\sigma_{n}^{z}\rangle,\@add@centering

(C.70)

where, for XXZ,
N
denotes the number of chain sites. Since XXZ is integrable, we do not necessarily assume that all
\langle\sigma_{n}^{z}\rangle
are similar, but it is anyway clear from (
C.70
) that in general they need not be zero. This might make one think that the XXZ calculations should be re-made taking as an input the subtracted version of on-site Pauli matrices. Figure
C.3
illustrates that, in this case, subtracting the one-point function does not alter qualitatively the results because the connected part of the two-point function in this integrable system is already not exponentially suppressed, and hence the late-time value of the two-point function does not change drastically if one subtracts the disconnected part from it.

Figure C.3:
Long-time averaged transition probabilities for an instance of XXZ studied in
[
272
]
, this time also considering a version of the operator where the non-vanishing one-point function has been subtracted. In contrast to the cSYK
4
case, this time the subtraction of the one-point function does not alter drastically the K-complexity saturation value, since in this case undersaturation is due to the monotonously decaying profile of
Q_{0n}
that we associated to Anderson localization on the Krylov chain in
[
272
]
, together with the fact that even the connected part of the two-point function is itself not exponentially suppressed at late times due to the integrable nature of the system.

C.5
Profile of transition probability for flat operator

Consider a dense operator with constant matrix elements
O_{ab}=1
for all
a,b=1,\dots,D
. In the Krylov basis such an operator has the following profile

\displaystyle|\mathcal{O}_{0})=\Big(\underbrace{\frac{1}{D},\dots,\frac{1}{D}}_{\text{$\frac{D(D-1)}{2}$ terms}},\sqrt{\frac{1}{D}},\underbrace{\frac{1}{D},\dots,\frac{1}{D}}_{\text{$\frac{D(D-1)}{2}$ terms}}\Big)

(C.71)

such that
\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}|O_{i}|^{2}=1
. We will call such an operator a “flat” operator.

We recall from
[
272
]
that for an odd number of elements in the Krylov basis, which is the case when no degeneracies are present and the operator has a non-zero projection over all Liouvillian frequencies (in such a case
K=D^{2}-D+1
which is an odd number), the Liouvillian eigenvector at the middle of its spectrum has zero eigenvalue

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}|\omega_{\text{middle}})=\omega_{\text{middle}}|\omega_{\text{middle}})=0~.

(C.72)

In general, the Liovillian eigenvectors can be expanded in the Krylov basis:

\displaystyle|\omega_{i})=\sum_{n=0}^{K-1}\psi_{ni}|\mathcal{O}_{n})~.

(C.73)

For
|\omega_{\text{middle}})
the coefficients
\psi_{n}
satisfy

\displaystyle\psi_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{O}_{2n}|\omega_{\text{middle}})=\psi_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{b_{2i-1}}{b_{2i}}\equiv\psi_{0}X_{n}

(C.74)

\displaystyle\psi_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{O}_{2n+1}|\omega_{\text{middle}})=0~,

(C.75)

where in (
C.74
) we defined
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{b_{2i-1}}{b_{2i}}\equiv X_{n}
for later convenience.
Note that
\psi_{0}
is determined by the middle element of (
C.71
), i.e.

\displaystyle\psi_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{D}}

(C.76)

since in
\mathcal{L}
’s eigenvector matrix, the middle element of
|\mathcal{O}_{0})
is the first element in
|\omega_{\text{middle}})
as can be seen from (
C.73
).

With the information from (
C.71
), (
C.74
,
C.75
) and (
C.76
) we can compute
Q_{0n}
in terms of the Lanczos coefficients.
Starting with the definition (
4.19
)

\displaystyle Q_{0n}=\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}|(\mathcal{O}_{0}|\omega_{i})|^{2}|(\mathcal{O}_{n}|\omega_{i})|^{2}

(C.77)

the first element is given by

\displaystyle Q_{00}=\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}|(\mathcal{O}_{0}|\omega_{i})|^{4}=D(D-1)\frac{1}{D^{4}}+\frac{1}{D^{2}}=\frac{2D-1}{D^{3}}~.

(C.78)

where we used (
C.71
) directly. The next element is

\displaystyle Q_{01}=\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}|(\mathcal{O}_{0}|\omega_{i})|^{2}|(\mathcal{O}_{1}|\omega_{i})|^{2}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{i\neq\text{middle}}|(\mathcal{O}_{1}|\omega_{i})|^{2}+\frac{1}{D}\cdot 0=\frac{1}{D^{2}}

(C.79)

where in the second equality we used the fact that the Krylov elements are normalized, hence
1=\sum_{i\neq\text{middle}}|(\mathcal{O}_{1}|\omega_{i})|^{2}+|(\mathcal{O}_{1}|\omega_{\text{middle}})|^{2}
and since from (
C.75
),
(\mathcal{O}_{1}|\omega_{\text{middle}})=0
we deduce that
\sum_{i\neq\text{middle}}|(\mathcal{O}_{1}|\omega_{i})|^{2}=1
.

\displaystyle Q_{02}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}|(\mathcal{O}_{0}|\omega_{i})|^{2}|(\mathcal{O}_{2}|\omega_{i})|^{2}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{i\neq\text{middle}}|(\mathcal{O}_{2}|\omega_{i})|^{2}+\frac{1}{D}|(\mathcal{O}_{2}|\omega_{\text{middle}})|^{2}

(C.80)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D^{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{D}X_{1}^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{D}\left(\frac{1}{D}X_{1}^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\frac{X_{1}^{2}}{D^{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{D}\right)~.

The rest of
Q_{0n}
can be computed in a similar manner, and we conclude that for
n\geq 1

\displaystyle Q_{0,2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\frac{X_{n}^{2}}{D^{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{D}\right)

(C.81)

\displaystyle Q_{0,2n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D^{2}}~.

(C.82)

One can check that this result is normalized

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{K-1}Q_{0n}=\frac{2D-1}{D^{3}}+\left(\frac{K-1}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\left(\frac{K-1}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\frac{D-1}{D^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\frac{K-1}{2}}\frac{X_{n}^{2}}{D}=1

(C.83)

where we used
K=D^{2}-D+1
and from normalization of
|\omega_{\text{middle}})
we know that
\frac{1}{D}+\sum_{n=1}^{\frac{K-1}{2}}\frac{X_{n}^{2}}{D}=1
.
The value of K-complexity can then be estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\overline{C_{K}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{K-1}nQ_{0n}=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{K-1}n+\frac{D-1}{D^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\frac{K-1}{2}}2n\frac{X_{n}^{2}}{D}

(C.84)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}K(K-1)\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\frac{D-1}{D^{2}}{C_{K}}_{\text{middle}}

where
{C_{K}}_{\text{middle}}
is the K-complexity of the eigenvector
|\omega_{\text{middle}})
5
5

5

K-complexity for individual eigenvectors of the Liouvillian was defined in
[
272
]
.
and by definition
0\leq{C_{K}}_{\text{middle}}\leq K
. Hence it is found that for a flat operator

\displaystyle\frac{D^{2}}{2}-D+1-\frac{1}{2D}\leq\overline{C_{K}}\leq\frac{D^{2}}{2}-1+\frac{3}{2D}-\frac{1}{D^{2}}

(C.85)

which for large enough
D
indicates that

\displaystyle\overline{C_{K}}\sim\frac{D^{2}}{2}\sim\frac{K}{2}

(C.86)

independently of the spectrum or Lanczos coefficients data. We show numerically that this is indeed the case by studying flat operators with Hamiltonians of GOE statistics and Poissonian statistics in Figure
C.4
.

Figure C.4:
K-complexity saturation value for constant operator evolving under Hamiltonian taken from a GOE ensemble (left) and Hamiltonian with Poissonian statistics (right), both computed at
D=32
. Note that both cases exhibit saturation value close to
K/2
.

Appendix D
Appendices to Chapter
5

D.1
Coupling variance choices

For reference, this Appendix discusses the relation between the various choices of SYK coupling variance in the literature. Different choices are suitable depending on the specific limit in which the system is studied, as they will produce bounded results for observables such as expectation values of operators and correlation functions.

In
[
360
]
, the SYK model is studied analytically at large
N
, first at fixed
p
and later on at also large
p
. The Hamiltonian in that article is:

H^{(1)}=i^{p/2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<...<i_{p}\leq N}J^{(1)}_{i_{1}...i_{p}}\chi_{i_{1}}...\chi_{i_{p}},

(D.1)

where the Majoranas are normalized such that they square to
\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}
, that is:

\left\{\chi_{i},\chi_{j}\right\}=\delta_{ij}.

(D.2)

For the sake of studying the model in the large-
N
limit at fixed
p
, the coupling variance used in
[
360
]
is

\centering\left\langle\left(J_{i_{1}...i_{p}}^{(1)}\right)^{2}\right\rangle=\frac{(p-1)!J^{2}}{N^{p-1}}.\@add@centering

(D.3)

When
[
360
]
turns to studying SYK at large
N
and large
p
, the Hamiltonian keeps the same structure,

H^{(2)}=i^{p/2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<...<i_{p}\leq N}J^{(2)}_{i_{1}...i_{p}}\chi_{i_{1}}...\chi_{i_{p}},

(D.4)

but the coupling variance is chosen to have an extra
p
-dependent scaling to get a more uniform limit:

\left\langle\left(J_{i_{1}...i_{p}}^{(2)}\right)^{2}\right\rangle=\frac{2^{p-1}(p-1)!}{pN^{p-1}}J^{2}.

(D.5)

The origin of the
2^{p-1}
factor is related to the normalization of the Majoranas (
D.2
): Since they square to
\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}
, they will give an additional suppression of
2^{-p}
every time a Wick contraction pairs two Hamiltonians inside of a trace, which needs to be compensated by the coupling variance in order to obtain finite results in the large-
p
limit. More explicitly, redefining the Majoranas as

\chi_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\psi_{i},

(D.6)

we obtain operators
\psi_{i}
that are normalized in a way such that they square to one:

\left\{\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\right\}=2\delta_{ij}.

(D.7)

In terms of these operators, the Hamiltonian (
D.4
) reads:

H^{(2)}=i^{p/2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<...<i_{p}\leq N}J^{(2)}_{i_{1}...i_{p}}2^{-\frac{p}{2}}\psi_{i_{1}}...\psi_{i_{p}}\equiv i^{p/2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<...<i_{p}\leq N}\widetilde{J^{(2)}}_{i_{1}...i_{p}}\psi_{i_{1}}...\psi_{i_{p}},

(D.8)

so that the effective coupling strength variance gets modified to

\left\langle\left(\widetilde{J^{(2)}}_{i_{1}...i_{p}}\right)^{2}\right\rangle=\left\langle\left(J^{(2)}_{i_{1}...i_{p}}\right)^{2}\right\rangle 2^{-p}=\frac{(p-1)!}{2pN^{p-1}}J^{2}.

(D.9)

When turning to double-scaled SYK, extensively studied in
[
368
]
, it is more enlightening to work with normalized Majoranas
\psi_{i}
satisfying (
D.7
) since, as mentioned in section
5.2.1
, the monomials
\psi_{I}
, where
I
denotes a collective index
i_{1}...i_{p}
, square to a phase, and therefore the combinatorial factor coming from the sum in the Hamiltonian can be directly compensated by the normalization of the coupling strength in order to yield bounded moments of the Hamiltonian. The Hamiltonian in
[
368
]
is:

H^{(3)}=i^{p/2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<...<i_{p}\leq N}J^{(3)}_{i_{1}...i_{p}}\psi_{i_{1}}...\psi_{i_{p}},

(D.10)

and the coupling strength variance is given by

\left\langle\left(J^{(3)}_{i_{1}...i_{p}}\right)^{2}\right\rangle={\binom{N}{p}}^{-1}J^{2}.

(D.11)

As an example, this normalization ensures that
\left\langle\text{Tr}\left[\left(H^{(3)}\right)^{2}\right]\right\rangle=J^{2}
, even at finite and independent
N
and
p
.

For the developments in Chapter
5
, we are interested in double-scaled SYK but we do not use the coupling strength variance (
D.11
). Instead, we follow
[
369
,
538
]
and use a variance which, when taken in the double-scaling limit, has an asymptotic behavior that makes contact with (
D.9
) in a way about which we shall be precise below. Our Hamiltonian is:

H=i^{p/2}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<...<i_{p}\leq N}J_{i_{1}...i_{p}}\psi_{i_{1}}...\psi_{i_{p}}.

(D.12)

We study this system in the double-scaling limit, taking
N
and
p
to infinity keeping the ratio

\lambda=\frac{2p^{2}}{N}

(D.13)

fixed. Given
\lambda
, the coupling strength variance is chosen to be:

\left\langle J_{i_{1}...i_{p}}^{2}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\lambda}{\binom{N}{p}}^{-1}\,J^{2}.

(D.14)

To compare with (
D.9
), we can perform an asymptotic analysis already within the double-scaling limit. That is, we take (
D.14
) and implicitly assume everywhere that
N\equiv N(p)=\frac{2p^{2}}{\lambda}
. Then, for large
p
we find the asymptotic behavior:

\centering\left\langle J_{i_{1}...i_{p}}^{2}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\lambda}{\binom{N(p)}{p}}^{-1}\,J^{2}\sim\frac{e^{\lambda/4}}{2p}\frac{(p-1)!}{{N(p)}^{p-1}}J^{2}=\frac{(p-1)!}{2p{N(p)}^{p-1}}J^{2}+\mathit{O}(\lambda).\@add@centering

(D.15)

Where
f(p)\sim g(p)\Leftrightarrow\lim_{p\to+\infty}\frac{f(p)}{g(p)}=1
.
This means that, in the double-scaling limit, the variance (
D.14
) is equivalent to the choice (
D.9
) upon the additional assumption of small
\lambda
, which is precisely the regime on which papers like
[
369
,
538
]
focus, since it zooms in near the ground state of SYK and yields the Liouville Hamiltonian which makes contact with Schwarzian dynamics and hence with an AdS bulk dual.

In view of this, the small-
\lambda
regime of DSSYK can be seen as a controlled way to approach large-
N
and large-
p
SYK with the hierarchy
1\ll p\ll N
, as also discussed in
[
539
]
.

D.2
A note on the triple-scaling limit

A priori, there is a whole family of triple scaling limits that one can take which, applied to the Hamiltonian (
5.57
), yield Liouville-like Hamiltonians with a different relative weight between the kinetic term and the potential term. The family of limits is parametrized by some
a\in\mathds{R}
as follows:

\centering\lambda\to 0,\hskip 18.49988ptl\to\infty,\hskip 18.49988pt(a\lambda)^{-2}e^{-\frac{l}{l_{f}}}=e^{-\frac{\tilde{l}}{l_{f}}}\,\text{fixed}.\@add@centering

(D.16)

Applied to (
5.57
), this limit yields:

\centering\tilde{T}-E_{0}=2\lambda J\left(\frac{l_{f}^{2}k^{2}}{2}+\frac{a^{2}}{2}e^{-\frac{\tilde{l}}{l_{f}}}\right)\;+\mathit{O}\left(\lambda^{2}\right).\@add@centering

(D.17)

However, all the Hamiltonians in the family (
D.17
) are equivalent in the sense that they only differ by a finite translation of the length variable. Their density of states is therefore the same and the eigenfunctions of one Hamiltonian in the family can be obtained from those of any other by just a shift. In our work, we choose
a=2
(instead of the
a=1
convention in
[
369
]
) because in this case the resulting triple-scaled Hamiltonian makes contact with the form of the Liouville Hamiltonian for JT gravity written explicitly in
[
519
]
, restated in (
5.51
). Nevertheless, we stress that, since all the Hamiltonians in the family (
D.17
) are equivalent in the sense that we have just explained, changing
a
in the definition of the triple-scaling limit (
D.16
) will not modify the gravity theory fundamentally. This ambiguity in the definition of
\tilde{l}
may be seen as related to the choice of regularization scheme for the boundary divergence from the bulk perspective.

D.3
Eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the effective Hamiltonian

The discussion in this Appendix closely follows
[
368
,
518
]
and is presented for the sake of completeness.

The symmetric version of
T
given in (
5.12
) satisfies, in the chord basis,

T\,|l\rangle=\frac{J}{\sqrt{\lambda(1-q)}}\left(\sqrt{1-q^{l+1}}\,|l+1\rangle+\sqrt{1-q^{l}}\,|l-1\rangle\right)~.

(D.18)

To find the eigenvalues of
T
we write down the eigensystem equation for the components of the eigenvectors of
T
,
\psi_{l}(E)=\langle l|E\rangle
:

E\,\psi_{l}(E)=\frac{J}{\sqrt{\lambda(1-q)}}\left(\sqrt{1-q^{l+1}}\,\psi_{l+1}(E)+\sqrt{1-q^{l}}\,\psi_{l-1}(E)\right)

(D.19)

We note that
E
is required to be independent of the position label
l
as (
D.19
) is an eigenvalue problem; therefore, assuming there are no bound states, we can take the large
l
limit of the above relation where
T
becomes a tridiagonal Toeplitz
1
1

1

A Toeplitz matrix is a matrix with equal entries along the diagonals.
matrix with eigensystem equation

E\,\psi_{l}(E)=\frac{J}{\sqrt{\lambda(1-q)}}\left(\psi_{l+1}(E)+\psi_{l-1}(E)\right)~.

(D.20)

The eigenvalues of such an
L
-dimensional tridiagonal Toeplitz matrix are given by:

E_{k}=\frac{2J}{\sqrt{\lambda(1-q)}}\cos\left(\frac{k\pi}{L+1}\right),\hskip 9.24994ptk=1,\dots,L~.

(D.21)

When
L
is large the argument of the cosine,
\theta=\frac{k\pi}{L+1}
, becomes a continuous variable between
0
and
\pi
and the spectrum has values between
E_{min}=-\frac{2J}{\lambda\sqrt{1-q}}
and
E_{max}=\frac{2J}{\lambda\sqrt{1-q}}
. The eigenvalues are then a function of the continuous variable
2
2

2

When
l
becomes continuous,
\theta
can be interpreted as momentum.

\theta
:

E(\theta)=\frac{2J\,\cos\theta}{\sqrt{\lambda(1-q)}},\hskip 9.24994pt0\leq\theta\leq\pi~.

(D.22)

Note that as
L\to\infty
, the variable
\theta
becomes continuous in a uniform way and thus summing over eigenvalues
\sum_{k=1}^{L}
becomes
\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta
with uniform density
\rho(\theta)=1
. This fact will be important for later results.

To find the eigenvectors, we go back to (
D.19
) using the result (
D.22
) and defining for convenience
\mu=\cos\theta
(we will use
v_{l}
instead of
\psi_{l}
, reserving the latter to the final normalized result):

2\mu\,v_{l}(\mu)=\sqrt{1-q^{l+1}}\,v_{l+1}(\mu)+\sqrt{1-q^{l}}\,v_{l-1}(\mu)~.

(D.23)

This equation can be identified once
v_{l}(\mu)
is redefined as follows

v_{l}(\mu)=\frac{\tilde{v}_{l}(\mu)}{\sqrt{(q;q)_{l}}}

(D.24)

where
(a;q)_{n}\equiv\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(1-aq^{k})
is the
q
-Pochhammer symbol. Plugging this redefinition into (
D.23
) and multiplying both sides of the equation by
\sqrt{(q;q)_{l}}
gives:

2\mu\tilde{v}_{l}(\mu)=(1-q^{l})\tilde{v}_{l-1}(\mu)+\tilde{v}_{l+1}(\mu)

(D.25)

which now can be identified as the recurrence relation for the
q-Hermite polynomials
(see Appendix
D.3.1
), hence the solution to (
D.23
) is

v_{l}(\mu)=\frac{H_{l}(\mu|q)}{\sqrt{(q;q)_{l}}},\hskip 9.24994pt\mu=\cos(\theta)~.

(D.26)

Using the identity (
D.37
) and the fact that
0\leq\theta\leq\pi
, it can be shown that the norm squared of these eigenvectors is given by

\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}|v_{l}(\mu)|^{2}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{l}(\mu|q)H_{l}(\mu|q)}{(q;q)^{l}}=\frac{2\pi}{|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|^{2}(q;q)_{\infty}}~,

(D.27)

and therefore the normalized eigenvector is given by

\psi_{l}(\mu)=\sqrt{(q;q)_{\infty}}|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|\frac{H_{l}(\mu|q)}{\sqrt{2\pi(q;q)_{l}}},\hskip 9.24994pt\mu=\cos\theta~.

(D.28)

We note that

\psi_{0}(\mu)=\sqrt{(q;q)_{\infty}}|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|\frac{H_{0}(\mu|q)}{\sqrt{2\pi(q;q)_{0}}}=\sqrt{\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{2\pi}}|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|

(D.29)

where we used
H_{0}(x|q)=1
and
(q;q)_{0}=1
. We thus can write

\psi_{l}(\mu)=\psi_{0}(\mu)\frac{H_{l}(\mu|q)}{\sqrt{(q;q)_{l}}},\hskip 9.24994pt\mu=\cos\theta.

(D.30)

The orthogonality relations for these eigenvectors are shown in Appendix
D.3.3
.

D.3.1
q-Hermite Polynomials

The q-Hermite polynomial is defined as
[
530
]
:

H_{n}(\cos\theta|q)=e^{in\theta}{}_{2}\phi_{0}\Big[\begin{matrix}q^{-n},0\\
-\end{matrix};\;q,q^{n}e^{-2i\theta}\Big]

(D.31)

where
{}_{2}\phi_{0}
is a q-hypergeometric function, defined by
[
530
]
:

{}_{j}\phi_{k}\Big[\begin{matrix}a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{j}\\
b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{k}\end{matrix};\;q,z\Big]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a_{1};q)_{n}\,(a_{2};q)_{n}\,\dots(a_{j};q)_{n}}{(b_{1};q)_{n}\,(b_{2};q)_{n}\,\dots(b_{k};q)_{n}}\Big[(-1)^{n}q^{n(n-1)/2}\Big]^{1+k-j}\frac{z^{n}}{(q;q)_{n}}~.

(D.32)

Using this definition we have

H_{n}(\cos\theta|q)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(q^{-n};q)_{k}}{(q;q)_{k}}(-1)^{k}q^{nk-\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}e^{i(n-2k)\theta}

(D.33)

Now note that,
(q^{-n};q)_{k}=(1-q^{-n})(1-q^{-n+1})(1-q^{-n+2})\dots(1-q^{-n+k-1})
so for
k=n+1
we have
(q^{-n};q)_{n+1}=(1-q^{-n})(1-q^{-n+1})(1-q^{-n+2})\dots(1-1)=0
. We thus find that for
(q^{-n};q)_{k>n+2}=0
and the sum becomes truncated at
k=n
:

H_{n}(\cos\theta|q)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(q^{-n};q)_{k}}{(q;q)_{k}}(-1)^{k}q^{nk-\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}e^{i(n-2k)\theta}~.

(D.34)

From this expression we can get another useful expression for
H_{n}(\cos\theta|q)
, as follows:

\displaystyle(q^{-n};q)_{k}

\displaystyle=(1-q^{-n})(1-q^{-n+1})(1-q^{-n+2})\dots(1-q^{-n+k-1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}(q^{-n}-1)(q^{-n+1}-1)(q^{-n+2}-1)\dots(q^{-n+k-1}-1)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\,q^{-n}(1-q^{n})\,q^{-n+1}(1-q^{n+1}))\dots q^{-n+k-1}(1-q^{n-k+1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\,q^{-nk+\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(q;q)_{n-k}}~,

and thus, another expression for the q-Hermite polynomial is

H_{n}(\cos\theta|q)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(q;q)_{k}\,(q;q)_{n-k}}e^{i(n-2k)\theta}~.

(D.35)

D.3.2
Orthogonality relations

A useful identity for q-Hermite polynomials with
x=\cos\theta,y=\cos\phi
(see for example
[
540
]
) is:

\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}H_{l}(x|q)H_{l}(y|q)\frac{t^{l}}{(q;q)_{l}}=\frac{(t^{2};q)_{\infty}}{(te^{i(\theta+\phi)};q)_{\infty}(te^{i(\theta-\phi)};q)_{\infty}(te^{-i(\theta-\phi)};q)_{\infty}(te^{-i(\theta+\phi)};q)_{\infty}},

(D.36)

which for
t=1
and
x=y
is exactly the needed identity.
Noting that

(te^{i(\theta+\phi)};q)_{\infty}(te^{-i(\theta+\phi)};q)_{\infty}=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-te^{i(\theta+\phi)}q^{k})(1-te^{-i(\theta+\phi)}q^{k})=|(te^{i(\theta+\phi)};q)_{\infty}|^{2}~,

and similarly for
e^{i(\theta-\phi)}
, we have:

\frac{(t^{2};q)_{\infty}}{|(te^{i(\theta+\phi)};q)_{\infty}|^{2}|(te^{i(\theta-\phi)};q)_{\infty}|^{2}}=\frac{(1-t^{2})(qt^{2};q)_{\infty}}{|1-te^{i(\theta+\phi)}|^{2}|1-te^{i(\theta-\phi)}|^{2}|(qte^{i(\theta+\phi)};q)_{\infty}|^{2}|(qte^{i(\theta-\phi)};q)_{\infty}|^{2}},

where it is made clear that for
t=1
the expression vanishes for all
\theta,\phi
except for
\theta=\pm\phi
. Taking
t=1-\epsilon
, in the limit
\epsilon\to 0

\theta\to\phi
we have

\displaystyle|1-te^{i(\theta-\phi)}|^{2}

\displaystyle=1-t[e^{i(\theta-\phi)}+e^{-i(\theta-\phi)}]+t^{2}

\displaystyle=1-t[1+i(\theta-\phi)-\frac{(\theta-\phi)^{2}}{2}+1-i(\theta-\phi)-\frac{(\theta-\phi)^{2}}{2}+\dots]+t^{2}

\displaystyle=1-(1-\epsilon)[2-(\theta-\phi)^{2}+O((\theta-\phi)^{4})]+(1-\epsilon)^{2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{2}+(\theta-\phi)^{2}+O(\epsilon(\theta-\phi)^{2})+\dots

and thus

\displaystyle\frac{(1-t^{2})(qt^{2};q)_{\infty}}{|1-te^{i(\theta+\phi)}|^{2}|1-te^{i(\theta-\phi)}|^{2}|(qte^{i(\theta+\phi)};q)_{\infty}|^{2}|(qte^{i(\theta-\phi)};q)_{\infty}|^{2}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{2\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2}+(\theta-\phi)^{2}}\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|^{2}|(q;q)_{\infty}|^{2}}~.

In the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, the first term is recognized as a delta function
\delta(t)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2}+t^{2}}
and taking into account also the possibility
\theta\to-\phi
it is found that

\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{l}(x|q)H_{l}(y|q)}{(q;q)_{l}}=\frac{2\pi[\delta(\theta-\phi)+\delta(\theta+\phi)]}{|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|^{2}(q;q)_{\infty}}~.

(D.37)

D.3.3
Orthogonality of the eigenvectors of DSSYK

In this Appendix we show that the eigenvectors (
D.30
) satisfy orthogonality relations in energy and in chord number. Firstly we check orthogonality in energy:

\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\psi_{l}(\cos\theta)\psi_{l}(\cos\phi)=\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{2\pi}|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|^{2}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{l}(\cos\theta|q)H_{l}(\cos\phi|q)}{(q;q)_{l}}=\delta(\theta-\phi)~,

(D.38)

where we used the result (
D.37
) together with the knowledge that both
\theta
and
\phi
get values between
0
and
\pi
.
Secondly, we check orthogonality in chord number:

\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\,\psi_{n}(\cos\theta)\psi_{m}(\cos\theta)=\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{\sqrt{(q;q)_{n}(q;q)_{m}}}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|^{2}H_{n}(\cos\theta|q)H_{m}(\cos\theta|q)~.

(D.39)

To compute this we need the following identity:

\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}|(e^{2i\theta};q)_{\infty}|^{2}H_{n}(\cos\theta|q)H_{m}(\cos\theta|q)=\frac{(q;q)_{n}}{(q;q)_{\infty}}\delta_{nm}~,

(D.40)

which gives immediately

\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\,\psi_{n}(\cos\theta)\psi_{m}(\cos\theta)=\delta_{nm}~.

(D.41)

D.4
Continuum approximation of Krylov space

This section provides a derivation of the continuum approximation of Krylov space developed in
[
379
]
in order to understand better its applicability and its relation to ballistic propagation.

In the case in which there are no diagonal Lanczos coefficients,
a_{n}=0
, it is useful to redefine the wavefunctions as

\centering\phi_{n}(t)=i^{n}\varphi_{n}(t),\@add@centering

(D.42)

so that
\varphi_{n}(t)\in\mathds{R}
, satisfying the recurrence relation:

\centering\dot{\varphi}_{n}(t)=b_{n}\varphi_{n-1}(t)-b_{n+1}\varphi_{n+1}(t),\@add@centering

(D.43)

for all
n=0,...,K-1
and with initial condition
\varphi_{n}(0)=\delta_{n,0}
. Note that, for the sake of rigor, we start by considering a finite Krylov space of dimension
K
; we shall eventually consider the limit of large
K
. For consistency,
b_{0}\equiv 1
and
\varphi_{-1}(t)\equiv 0
.

We will start by performing some exact manipulations that amount to rewriting (
D.43
) in a more suggestive form. Since Krylov space is discrete, position is labelled by
n=0,...,K-1
and the lattice spacing is constant and equal to
1
. Thus, strictly speaking, it does not go to zero in any sense. In order to make a continuum limit possible, we use a change of variables
\xi=\frac{n}{K-1}\equiv\varepsilon n
, where we identify the small parameter
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{K-1}
. As a result, position in Krylov space is denoted by a rescaled variable
\xi
which takes discrete values contained in the interval
[0,1]
:

\centering\xi\in\left\{\frac{n}{K-1},\;n=0,1,...,K-1\right\}=\left\{\varepsilon n,\;n=0,1,...,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\}.\@add@centering

(D.44)

The spacing between the allowed values of
\xi
is
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{K-1}
, and we note that in the limit
K\to+\infty
they accumulate to
\xi\in[0,1]
, hence yielding a continuum limit. But let us still keep
K
fixed.

An important technicality has to be considered before passing to the continuum limit. We need to assume that the Lanczos coefficients
b_{n}
are given by the evaluation at discrete values of the domain of a certain smooth function that we loosely denote
b(n)
. This has to be assumed, since analytic continuation cannot be invoked because for it to apply one needs to have a function defined over at least an interval, not a discrete domain. Our Lanczos coefficients for DSSYK (
5.71
) certainly satisfy this assumption, but there are examples in the literature of Lanczos sequences that do not fulfill it: the works
[
355
,
395
,
447
]
have studied systems where the Lanczos sequence shows staggering, the even and odd coefficients following different profiles. For such systems, the Lanczos sequence does not have a well-defined continuum limit.

Upon the previous variable rescaling, we have:

\centering b_{n}=b(n)=b\big((K-1)\xi\big)=:w(\xi),\@add@centering

(D.45)

where we have introduced the function
w
, defined over the rescaled compact domain (
D.44
).

Having assumed that the Lanczos coefficients
b_{n}
are given by evaluating a smooth function
b(x)
at discrete values
x=n
, it is now justified to make the same assumption for
\varphi_{n}(t)=\varphi(t,n)
. Then again, upon the variable rescaling:

\centering\varphi_{n}(t)=\varphi(t,n)=\varphi\big(t,(K-1)\xi\big)=:f(t,\xi),\@add@centering

(D.46)

where the domain for the second variable of
f
is (
D.44
). We can now rewrite (
D.43
) as:

\centering\partial_{t}f(t,\xi)=w(\xi)f(t,\xi-\varepsilon)-w(\xi+\varepsilon)f(t,\xi+\varepsilon).\@add@centering

(D.47)

We emphasize that (
D.47
) is an exact rewriting of the recurrence (
D.43
), with
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{K-1}
. A solution
f(t,\xi)
of (
D.47
) gives a solution for (
D.43
) by evaluating
\varphi_{n}(t)=f(t,\frac{n}{K-1})
.

Expanding (
D.47
), which we can do since we have assumed that
w(\xi)
is smooth and we have argued that
f(t,\xi)
can also be taken to be so, we arrive at

\centering\partial_{t}f(t,\xi)+v(\xi)\partial_{\xi}f(t,\xi)+\frac{1}{2}v^{\prime}(\xi)f(t,\xi)=\mathit{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),\@add@centering

(D.48)

where we have defined
v(\xi):=2\varepsilon w(\xi)
. The leading term of (
D.48
) is a chiral wave equation with velocity field
v(\xi)
and mass term
\frac{1}{2}v^{\prime}(\xi)f(t,\xi)
. This equation can be solved using a change of variables
\xi\mapsto\chi
such that
\chi=0
when
\xi=0
and
\frac{d\xi}{v(\xi)}=d\chi
. Since
v(\xi)
is of order
\varepsilon
, this change of variables unwraps the compact domain of
\xi
and has again a non-compact domain, but the discussion about the compact domain was technically necessary in order to explain in what sense there is a continuum limit, and why
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{K-1}
controls the deviation from it. Introducing additionally the function

\centering g(t,\chi):=\sqrt{v(\xi(\chi))}f(t,\xi(\chi)),\@add@centering

(D.49)

equation (
D.47
) simplifies further into:

\centering\left(\partial_{t}+\partial_{\chi}\right)g(t,\chi)=\mathit{O}(\varepsilon^{5/2}),\@add@centering

(D.50)

where we remind that
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{K-1}
. In the limit
K\to\infty
equation (
D.50
) becomes exactly a chiral wave equation with unit velocity for the wave function
g
with coordinates
t,\,\chi
. However, this does not imply that the continuum approximation of Krylov space becomes exact and indistinguishable from the discrete problem in the large-
K
limit: The intermediate steps of rescaling
n
to a compact domain
\xi=\varepsilon n
fail strictly speaking when
\varepsilon=0
, and the continuum approximation need not apply in general. It may be applicable for propagating wave packets whose typical length scale is larger than the lattice spacing, but in our cases of interest the initial condition for the discrete wave packet is
\varphi_{n}(t=0)=\delta_{n0}
, which therefore probes the discreteness of the Krylov chain at early times.

Interestingly, studying (
D.50
) we note that this continuum approximation behaves as a classical limit, because it results in ballistic propagation, as we shall explain. Given an initial condition
g(0,\chi)=g_{0}(\chi)
, which in our case will be proportional to a delta function centered at
\chi=0
, the generic solution of (
D.50
) simply propagates this packet to the right, without spreading it:

\centering g(t,\chi)=g_{0}(\chi-t).\@add@centering

(D.51)

Since the packet
g(t,\chi)
is a propagating delta function, K-complexity coincides with the position of its peak, which propagates at velocity
1
in
\chi
-space. Undoing the change of variables:

\centering t=\int_{0}^{\chi(t)}d\chi=\int_{0}^{\xi(t)}\frac{d\xi}{v(\xi)},\@add@centering

(D.52)

and using
v(\xi)=2\varepsilon w(\xi)=2\varepsilon b(\xi/\varepsilon)
, together with
\xi=\varepsilon n
, we reach the equation
3
3

3

José Barbón pointed out this expression in discussions prior to the publication of
[
274
]
.
:

\centering t=\int_{0}^{n(t)}\frac{dn}{2b(n)},\@add@centering

(D.53)

which can be used as an estimate for the position of the peak
n(t)
in
n
-space, in an approximation in which
n
is directly promoted to be a continuous variable.

We remark that the continuum approximation gives ballistic propagation as an output, rather than it being an additional, independent assumption. In this sense, this approximation can be thought of as a classical approximation, because the propagation of the wave packet has turned into the problem of the propagation of a localized particle with well-defined position that travels driven by a velocity field
2b(n)
in
n
-space.

D.5
Wavefunctions and Krylov complexity for specific values of q

In this Appendix we evaluate the exact wavefunctions,
\phi_{n}(t)
, for
q=0
and for
q=1
. For
q=0
we can use the result (
5.111
) by plugging
q=0
directly into the equation. For
q=1
we start from the eigensystem equation with Lanczos coefficients
b_{n}\sim\sqrt{n}
and find the eigenvectors in order to compute
\phi_{n}(t)
. The analysis of
q\to 1
is done in the main text.

D.5.1
Exact wavefunctions and K-complexity for q=0

For
q\to 0
the Lanczos coefficients are given by
b_{n}\sim\frac{J}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\equiv b
(
\to 0
) for all
n=0,1,2,\dots
. When the tridiagonal matrix with these Lanczos coefficients above and below the diagonal is a Liouvillian, the result for
\phi_{n}(t)
was found in
[
379
]
to be

\phi_{n}(t)=i^{n}\varphi_{n}(t)=i^{n}\frac{(n+1)}{bt}J_{n+1}(2bt)~,

(D.54)

whereas in our case (dynamics in the Schrödinger picture) we need to replace
t\to-t
.
We can arrive at this result from (
5.111
) by setting
q=0
:

\displaystyle(0;0)_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-0\cdot 0^{k})=1=(0;0)_{n}

(D.55)

\displaystyle(e^{2i\theta};0)_{\infty}

\displaystyle=1-e^{2i\theta}\Rightarrow|(e^{2i\theta};0)_{\infty}|^{2}=4\sin^{2}\theta

(D.56)

\displaystyle H_{n}(\cos\theta|0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(0;0)_{n}}{(0;0)_{k}(0;0)_{n-k}}e^{i(n-2k)\theta}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}e^{i(n-2k)\theta}=\frac{\sin[(1+n)\theta]}{\sin\theta}~,

(D.57)

where we used (
D.35
) to evaluate
H_{n}(\cos\theta|0)
. Plugging all this into (
5.111
) we have

\displaystyle\phi_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta e^{-2ibt\cos\theta}\sin\theta\sin[(1+n)\theta]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\,e^{-2ibt\cos\theta}\cos(n\,\theta)-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\,e^{-2ibt\cos\theta}\cos[(n+2)\,\theta]

\displaystyle=i^{n}J_{n}(-2bt)+i^{n+2}J_{n+2}(-2bt)=i^{n}\Big[J_{n}(-2bt)+J_{n+2}(-2bt)\Big]

\displaystyle=i^{n}\frac{(n+1)}{(-bt)}J_{n+1}(-2bt)~,

(D.58)

where in the second line we used the trigonometric identity
\sin\alpha\sin\beta=\frac{1}{2}[\cos(\alpha-\beta)-\cos(\alpha+\beta)]
, in the third line we used one of the integral representations of the Bessel function of the first kind
4
4

4

https://dlmf.nist.gov/10.9
(last consulted on 01/01/2024).

J_{n}(z)=\frac{{i^{-n}}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\,e^{iz\cos\theta}\cos(n\theta)
, and in the fourth line we used the Bessel function identity
[
530
]

J_{n}(z)+J_{n+2}(z)=\frac{2(n+1)}{z}J_{n+1}(z)
. We managed to recover the result (
D.54
) with
t\to-t
as required.

With the wavefunction given by (
D.58
) the result for Krylov complexity can be evaluated exactly as follows:

\displaystyle C_{K}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n|\phi_{n}(t)|^{2}=\frac{1}{(bt)^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n(n+1)^{2}J^{2}_{n+1}(-2bt)=\frac{1}{(bt)^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(n-1)n^{2}J_{n}^{2}(2bt)

\displaystyle=\frac{16}{3}(bt)^{2}[J_{0}^{2}(2bt)+J_{1}^{2}(2bt)]+J_{0}^{2}(2bt)+\frac{1}{3}J_{1}^{2}(2bt)-\frac{8}{3}btJ_{0}(2bt)J_{1}(2bt)-1.

(D.59)

Using the asymptotic forms of
J_{0}
and
J_{1}
,

\displaystyle J_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi x}}\cos(x-1/4\pi)~,

(D.60)

\displaystyle J_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi x}}\sin(x-1/4\pi)~,

(D.61)

gives

\displaystyle C_{K}(t)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\frac{16}{3\pi}bt+\frac{4}{3\pi}\cos(4bt)-1+O(1/t)~,

(D.62)

which is linear in
t
for large
t
.

D.5.2
Exact wavefunctions for q=1

For
q\to 1
the Lanczos coefficients are given by
b_{n}\sim J\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda}}
, and the eigensystem equation is
T|v(E)\rangle=E|v(E)\rangle
. Re-defining the energy variable as
E=\frac{J}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\varepsilon
, the equation can be written component-wise as

\varepsilon\,v_{l}(\varepsilon)=\sqrt{l}\,v_{l-1}(\varepsilon)+\sqrt{l+1}\,v_{l+1}(\varepsilon)~.

(D.63)

In a similar manner to the general
q
case, we redefine the eigenvector components as

v_{l}(\varepsilon)=\frac{\tilde{v}_{l}(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{l!}}~,

(D.64)

which leads to the equation

\varepsilon\,\tilde{v}_{l}(\varepsilon)=l\,\tilde{v}_{l-1}(\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{l+1}(\varepsilon)~.

(D.65)

This equation is solved by the (regular) Hermite polynomials given by
H_{e_{n}}(x)=2^{-n/2}H_{n}(x/\sqrt{2})
. We thus have

v_{l}(\varepsilon)=\frac{H_{e_{l}}(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{l!}}=\frac{1}{2^{l/2}\sqrt{l!}}H_{l}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\right)~.

(D.66)

As in the q-Hermite case, we will use Hermite identities to normalize the eigenvectors.

\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}v_{l}(\varepsilon_{1})v_{l}(\varepsilon_{2})=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{l}(\varepsilon_{1}/\sqrt{2})H_{l}(\varepsilon_{2}/\sqrt{2})}{2^{l}\,l!}=\sqrt{2\pi}e^{\frac{\varepsilon_{1}^{2}}{2}}\delta(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}),

(D.67)

where we used the identity
[
530
]
:
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{n}(x)H_{n}(y)}{2^{n}\,n!}=\sqrt{\pi}e^{\frac{1}{2}(x^{2}+y^{2})}\delta(x-y)
with
x=y=\varepsilon/\sqrt{2}
(using
\delta((\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})/\sqrt{2})=\sqrt{2}\delta(\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2})
). The normalized eigenvectors are then given by

\psi_{l}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{1/4}2^{l/2}\sqrt{l!}}\,e^{-\varepsilon^{2}/4}\,H_{l}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\right)~.

(D.68)

This result can be used directly to find the result (
5.86
) for the wavefunction in the case
q=1
:

\displaystyle\phi_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\varepsilon\,e^{-i\frac{J}{\sqrt{\lambda}}t\varepsilon}\psi_{l}(\varepsilon)\psi_{0}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,2^{l/2}\sqrt{l!}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\varepsilon\,e^{-i\frac{J}{\sqrt{\lambda}}t\varepsilon}\,e^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}}\,H_{l}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\right)

(D.69)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{(\gamma t)^{2}}{2}}\frac{(-i\,\gamma t)^{l}}{\sqrt{l!}},

(D.70)

where
\gamma=\frac{J}{\sqrt{\lambda}}
. Here, the identity
[
530
]

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,e^{-(z-x)^{2}}H_{n}(x)=2^{n}\sqrt{\pi}z^{n}
, was used. We have also assumed that the density of states is flat in
\varepsilon
; this has not been proved here, but it can be checked a posteriori by noting that the orthonormal eigenvectors (
D.68
) indeed satisfy the completeness relation with respect to the flat measure
d\varepsilon
.
